-algebraic subgroups, hence they are equal which proves in particular that
\mathbf{P}_{Z,s}=\mathbf{P}_{Z^{\prime},s}
as subgroups of
\mathbf{P}_{s}
. We have shown that
(\mathbf{P}_{Z},D_{\mathbf{P}_{Z}})=(\mathbf{P}_{Z^{\prime}},D_{\mathbf{P}_{Z^{\prime}}})
. Since
Z
was assumed to be special, it follows that
Z=Z^{\prime}
. Therefore
Z^{\mathrm{an}}
is a complex-analytic irreducible component of
\Phi^{-1}(\phi_{i,\Gamma}(\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{P}_{Z},D_{\mathbf{P}_{Z}}),\Gamma_{\mathbf{P}_{Z}}})
which proves the "if" part of the statement.
∎

(A)typical and transverse special subvarieties

Recollections on transverse intersections

We start by introducing some language to work with transverse intersections, which will be useful in the proofs of the main results. First recall the following standard statement in complex geometry

Lemma 4.12

(
[
30
, Thm. II.6.2, Prop. II.4.32]
)
.

Let
X,Y
be smooth and irreducible locally closed complex-analytic subsets of a complex manifold
Z
. Let
W
be a complex-analytic irreducible component of
X\cap Y
. Then:

(i)

W
is an irreducible locally closed complex-analytic subset of
Z
;

(ii)

\dim W\geqslant\dim X+\dim Y-\dim Z
;

(iii)

if
\dim W=\dim X+\dim Y-\dim Z
, then
W
is smooth.

In the setting of Lemma
4.12
(iii) we say that
X
and
Y
intersect transversally in
Z
along
W
.
There is a differential theoretic definition of this notion:

Definition 4.13
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Z
be a smooth map between smooth manifolds, let
x\in X
and let
Y
be a submanifold of
Z
. The map
f
is said to be
transverse to
Y
at
x
if

T_{f(x)}Y+\mathrm{im}\hskip 1.42271pt(d_{x}f)=T_{f(x)}Z.

The following is immediate from the definitions:

Lemma 4.14

(
[
34
, Chap. I,§5.]
)
.

Let
X,Y
be smooth and irreducible locally closed complex-analytic subsets of a complex manifold
Z
, and let
x\in X\cap Y
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

there exists a complex-analytic irreducible component
W
of
X\cap Y
containing
x
such that
X
and
Y
intersect transversally along
W
in
Z
;

(ii)

the inclusion
i:X_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow Z_{\mathbb{R}}
(seen as a smooth map between smooth manifolds) is transverse to
Y_{\mathbb{R}}
at
x
.

The interest of this differential theoretic formulation of transversality for this work comes from the following stability result:

Theorem 4.15

(
[
34
, Chap. 1,§6, Stability Theorem]
)
.

Let
X,S
and
Z
be smooth manifolds, let
Y
be a submanifold of
Z
and let
F:X\times S\rightarrow Z
be a smooth map. Let
\pi:X\times S\rightarrow S
be the projection to
S
, and for
s\in S
let
f_{s}:X\rightarrow Z,x\mapsto F(x,s)
. Assume that there exists a point
(x_{o},o)\in X\times S
such that
f_{o}
is transverse to
Y
at
x_{o}
. Then, there exists

(i)

an open neighborhood
\Omega
of
o
in
S
,

(ii)

a smooth map
\sigma:\Omega\rightarrow X
such that
\sigma(o)=x_{o}
,

such that for every
s\in\Omega
, the smooth map
f_{s}
is transverse to
Y
at
\sigma(s)
.

Proof.

Using
op. cit.
, it suffices to construct an open neighborhood
\Omega
of
o
and map
\sigma:\Omega\rightarrow X
with
\sigma(o)=x_{o}
such that for any
s\in S
one has
F(\sigma(s),s)\in Y
. For this, it suffices to show that the restriction of
\pi
to
F^{-1}(Y)
is a submersion at
(x_{o},o)
. This follows easily from a dimension count, using that
F
is transverse to
Y
at
(x_{o},o)
since
f_{o}
is transverse to
Y
at
x_{o}
.
∎

(A)typical special subvarieties and the Zilber-Pink Conjecture

Back to our original setting, let
Z
be a special subvariety of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
. Let
(\mathbf{P}_{Z},D_{\mathbf{P}_{Z}})\subsetneq(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
be its generic mixed Hodge class. By Proposition
4.11
one has that
\Phi(Z^{\mathrm{an}})
is a complex-analytic irreducible component of the intersection of the locally-closed complex-analytic subsets
\Phi(S^{\mathrm{an}})
and
\phi_{i,\Gamma}(\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{P}_{Z},D_{\mathbf{P}_{Z}}),\Gamma_{\mathbf{P}_{Z}}})
of the complex manifold
\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}}),\Gamma}
. The following definition was introduced in spirit in
[
39
]
, later refined by
[
9
]
in the pure case and finally appeared in the present form in
[
10
]
for the mixed setting:

Definition 4.16
.

A special subvariety
Z
of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
is called

(a)

atypical
if either
\Phi(Z^{\mathrm{an}})
is contained in the singular locus of
\Phi(S^{\mathrm{an}})
or

\dim\Phi(Z^{\mathrm{an}})>\dim\Phi(S^{\mathrm{an}})+\dim\phi_{i,\Gamma}(\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{P}_{Z},D_{\mathbf{P}_{Z}}),\Gamma_{\mathbf{P}_{Z}}}-\dim\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}}),\Gamma}.

(b)

typical
otherwise.

The union of atypical (resp. typical) special subvarieties of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
is called the
atypical
(resp.
typical
)
Hodge locus of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
and is denoted
\mathrm{HL}(S,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes})_{\mathrm{atyp}}
(resp.
\mathrm{HL}(S,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes})_{\mathrm{typ}}
).

With this definition in hand, we can state the main conjecture motivating this work:

Conjecture 4.17
.

Let
\mathbb{V}
be an admissible integral and graded-polarized variation of mixed Hodge structures on a smooth and irreducible quasi-projective complex algebraic variety
S
. Then

(a)

\mathrm{HL}(S,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes})_{\mathrm{atyp}}
is a Zariski-closed subset of
S
;

(b)

If
\mathrm{HL}(S,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes})_{\mathrm{typ}}
is non-empty, it is dense in
S
for the metric topology.

Part
(a)
of Conjecture
4.17
was stated in this generality in
[
10
]
, refining the statement in
[
39
]
. Part
(b)
is a direct generalization of a conjecture stated in
[
9
]
, and is the main object of study of the present work which gives strong evidence for its validity. In particular we show that
(a)
implies
(b)
.

Transverse special subvarieties

The purpose of this section is to define the notion of transverse special subvariety, a weakened notion of typicality, which appears to us to be the right setting to study density phenomena for Hodge loci (see Remark
4.21
). We start with the following:

Definition 4.18
.

Let
Z
be an irreducible algebraic subvariety of
S
and
(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}})
a mixed Hodge subclass of
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
. We say that
Z
is
defined by
(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}})
if it is a complex-analytic irreducible component of
\Phi^{-1}(\phi_{i,\Gamma}(\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}}),\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}})
.

Remark 4.19
.

By Proposition
4.11
, a special subvariety of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
is always defined by its generic mixed Hodge class, and an irreducible subvariety of
S
defined by a strict mixed Hodge subclass of
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
is special. We however emphasize on the fact that the usefulness of this notion is that a special subvariety of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
can also be defined by a Hodge subclass of
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
which contains strictly its generic Hodge class (see Remark
4.21
).

Definition 4.20
.

Let
Z
be a special subvariety of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
and
(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}})
a mixed Hodge subclass of
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
. We say that
Z
is
(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}})
-
transverse
if

•

\Phi(Z^{\mathrm{an}})
is not contained in the singular locus of
\Phi(S^{\mathrm{an}})
;

•

Z
is defined by
(\mathbf{M},D_{M})
;

•

\dim\Phi(S^{\mathrm{an}})+\dim D_{\mathbf{M}}-\dim D_{\mathbf{P}}=\dim\Phi(Z^{\mathrm{an}})
.

The
transverse Hodge locus of type
(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}})
is the union
\mathrm{HL}(S,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes},\mathbf{M})_{\mathrm{trans}}
of special subvarieties of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
which are
(p\mathbf{M}p^{-1},p\cdot D_{M})
-transverse for some
p\in\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{Q})^{+}
. The
(full) transverse Hodge locus
is

\mathrm{HL}(S,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes})_{\mathrm{trans}}=\bigcup_{(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}})\subsetneq(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})}\mathrm{HL}(S,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes},\mathbf{M})_{\mathrm{trans}}.

Remark 4.21
.

Obviously, a typical special subvariety of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
is automatically transverse but the resulting containment
\mathrm{HL}(S,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes})_{\mathrm{typ}}\subset\mathrm{HL}(S,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes})_{\mathrm{trans}}
is strict in general as a special subvariety defined transversally by some mixed Hodge datum
(\mathbf{M},D_{M})
may in principle have a generic mixed Hodge datum strictly smaller than
(\mathbf{M},D_{M})
(in which case it is atypical). However, assuming Conjecture
4.17
(a)
one has that
\mathrm{HL}(S,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes})_{\mathrm{typ}}
is dense in
S
for the Zariski (resp. metric) topology if and only if
\mathrm{HL}(S,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes})_{\mathrm{trans}}
is. Ultimately the necessity of introducing the notion of transverse special subvarieties comes from the lack of current knowledge on the distribution of
\mathrm{HL}(S,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes})_{\mathrm{atyp}}
, in particular its zero-dimensional part (see
[
10
]
for more detail).

Weakly special subvarieties and Ax-Schanuel

We now recollect the necessary notions and results about weakly special subvarieties. This is simply to set notations as there are very good accounts of the theory in the literature, see notably
[
10
]
for a thorough discussion of the Ax-Schanuel theorem.

Weakly special subvarieties as intersection loci

Let
\mathbb{V}
be an integral, graded-polarized and admissible variation of mixed Hodge structures on a smooth, irreducible and quasi-projective complex algebraic variety
S
. Let
i:Z\hookrightarrow S
be the inclusion of an irreducible subvariety. The
algebraic monodromy group of
Z
for
\mathbb{V}
at
s\in Z^{\mathrm{sm}}(\mathbb{C})
is the identity component
\mathbf{H}_{Z,s}
of the Zariski closure of the image of

r_{s}=\rho_{s}\circ i_{\ast}\circ n_{\ast}:\pi_{1}((Z^{\mathrm{nor}})^{\mathrm{an}},s)\rightarrow\pi_{1}(Z^{\mathrm{an}},s)\rightarrow\pi_{1}(S^{\mathrm{an}},s)\rightarrow\mathbf{GL}(\mathbb{V}_{s}),

where
\rho_{s}:\pi_{1}(S^{\mathrm{an}},s)\rightarrow\mathbf{GL}(\mathbb{V}_{s})
is the monodromy representation of
\mathbb{V}
at
s
and
n:Z^{\mathrm{nor}}\rightarrow Z
is the normalization of
Z
, which is one-to-one when restricted to
n^{-1}(Z^{\mathrm{sm}})
allowing to abuse notation by writing
s
for the
\mathbb{C}
-point of
Z^{\mathrm{nor}}
corresponding to
s\in Z^{\mathrm{sm}}(\mathbb{C})
. One easily checks that
\dim\mathbf{H}_{Z,s}
doesn’t depend on the choice of
s\in Z^{\mathrm{sm}}(\mathbb{C})
, and we denote it by
\dim\mathbf{H}_{Z}
. This allows to make sense of the following:

Definition 5.1
.

Let
Z\subset S
be a strict irreducible subvariety. It is called
weakly special
if for any intermediate (for the inclusion) irreducible subvariety
Z\subsetneq Z^{\prime}\subseteq S
, one has:

\dim\mathbf{H}_{Z}<\dim\mathbf{H}_{Z^{\prime}}.

Weakly special subvarieties have an intersection theoretic characterization similar to Proposition
4.11
which will be of constant use in Sect. 7. We recall it now.

Definition 5.2

(
[
32
, Def. 2.5]
)
.

Let
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
be a mixed Hodge class. A
weak Mumford-Tate subdomain
of
D_{\mathbf{P}}
is the
\mathbf{N}(\mathbb{R})^{+}\mathbf{N}^{u}(\mathbb{C})
-orbit of some
h\in D_{\mathbf{M}}
, where
(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}})
is a mixed Hodge subclass of
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
and
\mathbf{N}
is a normal
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup of
\mathbf{M}
whose radical is unipotent. The datum
((\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}}),\mathbf{N},h)
is called the
weak Hodge class
of the generic weak Mumford-Tate subdomain.

One has:

Proposition 5.3

(
[
32
, Prop. 2.6]
)
.

Let
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
be a mixed Hodge class. A generic weak Mumford-Tate subdomain of
D_{\mathbf{P}}
is a smooth closed complex-analytic subspace of
D_{\mathbf{P}}
.

Let
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
be a mixed Hodge class and
\mathbf{N}
be a normal subgroup of
\mathbf{P}
. Gao and Klingler define in
[
32
, Prop. 5.1]
a "quotient" mixed Hodge class
(\mathbf{P}/\mathbf{N},D_{\mathbf{P}}/\mathbf{N})
together with a holomorphic map
q_{\mathbf{N},D_{\mathbf{P}}}:D_{\mathbf{P}}\rightarrow D_{\mathbf{P}}/\mathbf{N}
such that each fiber of
q_{\mathbf{N},D_{\mathbf{P}}}
is an
\mathbf{N}(\mathbb{R})^{+}\mathbf{N}^{u}(\mathbb{C})
-orbit in
D_{\mathbf{P}}
, and conversely any
\mathbf{N}(\mathbb{R})^{+}\mathbf{N}^{u}(\mathbb{C})
-orbit of
D_{\mathbf{P}}
arises as a fiber of
q_{\mathbf{N},D_{\mathbf{P}}}
. Notably, one has:

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
be a mixed Hodge class and
(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}})
a mixed Hodge subclass with underlying inclusion
i:\mathbf{M}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{P}
. The weak Mumford-Tate subdomain
D
of
D_{\mathbf{P}}
with weak Hodge class
((\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}}),\mathbf{N},h)
is the fiber
q_{\mathbf{N},D_{\mathbf{M}}}^{-1}(q_{\mathbf{N},D_{\mathbf{M}}}(h))
of
q_{\mathbf{N},D_{\mathbf{M}}}
seen as a subset of
D_{\mathbf{P}}
through the natural inclusion
D_{\mathbf{M}}\hookrightarrow D_{\mathbf{P}}
.

Let
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
be a mixed Hodge class, let
(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}})
be a mixed Hodge subclass with underlying inclusion
i:\mathbf{M}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{P}
, let
\mathbf{N}
be a normal subgroup of
\mathbf{M}
whose radical is unipotent, let
\Gamma
be a neat arithmetic subgroup of
\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{Q})^{+}
and
\pi_{\Gamma}:D_{\mathbf{P}}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}}),\Gamma}
be the quotient by
\Gamma
. Then

\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}/\mathbf{N}}:=\Big[\Gamma\cap\mathbf{M}(\mathbb{Q})/\Gamma\cap\mathbf{N}(\mathbb{Q})\Big]\cap(\mathbf{M}/\mathbf{N})(\mathbb{Q})^{+}

is a neat arithmetic subgroup of
(\mathbf{M}/\mathbf{N})(\mathbb{Q})^{+}
(see
[
4
, Chap. III, §7]
). By
[
32
]
, the quotient
q_{\mathbf{N},D_{\mathbf{M}}}:D_{\mathbf{M}}\rightarrow D_{\mathbf{M}}/\mathbf{N}
descends to a holomorphic map
p_{\mathbf{N},D_{\mathbf{M}},\Gamma}:\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}}),\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{M}/\mathbf{N},D_{\mathbf{M}}/\mathbf{N}),\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}/\mathbf{N}}}
.

Definition 5.5
.

Let
\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}}),\Gamma}
be a mixed Hodge variety. A
weak Hodge subvariety
of
\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}}),\Gamma}
is a complex-analytic locally closed subset arising as the image under
\pi_{\Gamma}
of a weak Mumford-Tate subdomain of
D_{\mathbf{P}}
. We denote by
\mathscr{W}_{((\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}}),\mathbf{N},h)}
the image under
\pi_{\Gamma}
of the weak Mumford-Tate subdomain of
D_{\mathbf{P}}
with weak Hodge class
((\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}}),\mathbf{N},h)
.

As in Lemma
5.4
, one has:

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}}),\Gamma)}
be a mixed Hodge variety, let
(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}})
be a mixed Hodge subclass with underlying inclusion
i:\mathbf{M}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{P}
and a let
\mathbf{N}
be a normal subgroup of
\mathbf{M}
. Then
\mathscr{W}_{((\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}}),\mathbf{N},h)}
is the image under
\phi_{i,\Gamma}
of a fiber of
p_{\mathbf{N},D_{\mathbf{M}},\Gamma}
.

Record the following, which is a direct consequence of the theorem of the fixed part:

Lemma 5.7

(
[
21
, Thm. 7.12]
)
.

Let
Z\subsetneq S
be an irreducible subvariety whith generic mixed Hodge class is
(\mathbf{P}_{Z},D_{\mathbf{P}_{Z}})
, and denote by
i_{Z}:\mathbf{P}_{Z}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{P}
the inclusion in the generic Mumford-Tate domain. Let
\mathbf{H}_{Z}
be the algebraic monodromy group of
Z
, which is a normal subgroup of
\mathbf{P}_{Z}
whose radical is unipotent. Then, the set
p_{\mathbf{H}_{Z},D_{\mathbf{P}_{Z}},\Gamma}(\phi_{i_{Z},\Gamma}^{-1}(\Phi(Z^{\mathrm{an}})))
is a singleton
\{[h_{Z}]\}
.

We can now turn to the intersection-theoretic characterization of generic weakly special subvarieties of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
of
[
39
]
.

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
Z\subsetneq S
be an irreducible subvariety with generic mixed Hodge class
(\mathbf{P}_{Z},D_{\mathbf{P}_{Z}})
and denote by
i_{Z}:\mathbf{P}_{Z}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{P}
the inclusion in the generic Mumford-Tate domain. Then
Z
is a weakly special subvariety of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
if and only if
Z
is a complex-analytic irreducible component of
\Phi^{-1}(\mathscr{W}_{((\mathbf{P}_{Z},D_{\mathbf{P}_{Z}}),\mathbf{H}_{Z},h_{Z})})
.

Mixed Ax-Schanuel theorem

We now recall the statement of the Ax-Schanuel theorem for admissible and graded-polarized integral variations of mixed Hodge structure, which is a key ingredient to our proofs. It was first shown independently in
[
32
]
and
[
24
]
. Note that after these proofs, a remarkable new perspective on Ax-Schanuel type theorems was discovered in
[
12
]
yielding a new proof of this statement in
[
8
]
.

Start with some notations. Lemma
5.7
applied to
Z=S
ensures the existence of a point
h_{S}\in D_{\mathbf{P}}/\mathbf{H}_{S}
such that letting
D_{\mathbf{H}_{S}}:=q_{\mathbf{H}_{S}}^{-1}(h_{S})
, the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\widetilde{S^{\mathrm{an}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\Phi}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{S}}
\textstyle{D_{\mathbf{H}_{S}}\subset D_{\mathbf{P}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{\Gamma}}
\textstyle{S^{\mathrm{an}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}}),\Gamma}}

Theorem 5.9

(Ax-Schanuel Theorem)
.

Let
\Delta=S\times_{\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}}),\Gamma}}D_{\mathbf{H}_{S}}
and
W\subset S\times D_{\mathbf{H}_{S}}
be an algebraic subvariety. Let
p_{S}:S\times D_{\mathbf{H}_{S}}\rightarrow S
be the projection to
S
. If a complex-analytic irreducible component
U
of
\Delta\cap W
satisfies

\dim U>\dim\Delta+\dim W-\dim S\times D_{\mathbf{H}_{S}}

then
p_{S}(U)
is contained in a strict weakly special subvariety of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
.

Geometric Zilber-Pink

A remarkable use of the Ax-Schanuel theorem was the proof by Baldi-Urbanik of their Geometric Zilber-Pink theorem. We recall its general statement and explain how it relates to the Zilber-Pink conjecture.

Definition 5.10

(
[
10
]
)
.

A weakly special subvariety
Z
of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
is called
monodromically atypical
if

\dim\Phi(Z^{\mathrm{an}})>\dim\Phi(S^{\mathrm{an}})+\dim\mathscr{W}_{((\mathbf{P}_{Z},D_{\mathbf{P}_{Z}}),\mathbf{H}_{Z},h_{Z})}-\dim\mathscr{W}_{((\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}}),\mathbf{H},h_{S})}.

Furthermore, it is called
maximal
if it is maximal for the inclusion among monodromically atypical weakly special subvarieties of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
.

With this terminology in hand, Baldi-Urbanik’s Geometric Zilber-Pink theorem reads:

Theorem 5.11

(
[
10
]
)
.

There exists a finite set
\Sigma_{(S,\mathbb{V})}
of pairs
((\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}}),\mathbf{N})
where
(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}})
is a mixed Hodge subclass of
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
and
\mathbf{N}
is a normal subgroup of
\mathbf{M}
whose radical is unipotent, such that for any maximal monodromically atypical weakly special subvariety
Z
of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
there exists
((\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}}),\mathbf{N})\in\Sigma_{(S,\mathbb{V})}
such that
\mathbf{N}=\mathbf{H}_{Z}
as subgroups of
\mathbf{P}
.

This statement in particular contains finiteness statements for some classes of atypical special subvarieties of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
, which is the form in which geometric Zilber-Pink theorems first appeared (see for instance
[
48
, Thm. 1.3]
and
[
9
, Thm. 3.1]
). Let
\Sigma_{(S,\mathbb{V})}^{\mathrm{gen}}
be the set of triples
((\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}}),\mathbf{N})\in\Sigma_{(S,\mathbb{V})}
such that
(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}})=(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
and
\Sigma_{(S,\mathbb{V})}^{\mathrm{sp}}=\Sigma_{(S,\mathbb{V})}-\Sigma_{(S,\mathbb{V})}^{\mathrm{gen}}
.

Definition 5.12
.

A special subvariety
Z
of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
has
\mathbb{V}
-factorwise positive dimension
if for every
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}}),\mathbf{N})\in\Sigma{(S,\mathbb{V})}^{\mathrm{gen}}
the inequality

\dim\Phi_{/\mathbf{N}}(Z^{\mathrm{an}})>0

holds.

Remark 5.13
.

In particular if a special subvariety
Z
of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
satisfies
\dim\Phi_{/\mathbf{N}}(Z^{\mathrm{an}})>0
, it has
\mathbb{V}
-factorwise positive dimension. The latter has the advantage of being a condition which depends only on the generic Mumford-Tate group
\mathbf{P}
(which is why we used this notion in the introduction) but has the inconvenient of consisting in a priori infinitely many conditions.

We denote by
\mathrm{HL}(S,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes})_{\mathrm{atyp},\mathrm{f-pos}}
the union of special subvarieties of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
which are atypical and have
\mathbb{V}
-factorwise positive dimension. One then deduces the following finiteness result from Baldi-Urbanik’s Geometric Zilber-Pink theorem, which somehow motivates the name of the result: it gives a finiteness result for maximal atypical special subvarieties which are not constructed from atypical special points for sub-quotient variations.

Corollary 5.14
.

There exists a finite set
\{Z_{1},\cdots,Z_{l}\}
of (strict) special subvarieties of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
such that

\mathrm{HL}(S,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes})_{\mathrm{atyp},\mathrm{f-pos}}\subset\bigcup_{k=1}^{l}Z_{k}.

In particular
\mathrm{HL}(S,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes})_{\mathrm{atyp},\mathrm{f-pos}}
is not dense in
S
for the Zariski topology.

Proof.

Let
\{Z_{1},\cdots,Z_{l}\}
be the set of special subvarieties of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
arising as irreducible components of preimages under
\Phi
of Hodge subvarieties of
\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}},\Gamma)}
of the form
\pi_{\Gamma}(D_{\mathbf{M}})
with for
((\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}}),\mathbf{N})\in\Sigma_{(S,\mathbb{V})}^{\mathrm{sp}}
. These are indeed strict special subvarieties of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
by Proposition
4.11
, using the fact that for
((\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}}),\mathbf{N})\in\Sigma_{(S,\mathbb{V})}^{\mathrm{sp}}
, one has
(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}})\subsetneq(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
. Theey are in finite number because
\Sigma_{(S,\mathbb{V})}^{\mathrm{sp}}
is finite by Theorem
5.11
. We will prove that
\mathrm{HL}(S,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes})_{\mathrm{atyp},\mathrm{f-pos}}\subset\bigcup_{k=1}^{l}Z_{k}.

Let
Z
be an atypical special subvariety of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
which has
\mathbb{V}
-factorwise positive dimension. We need to show the existence of a
k\in\{1,\cdots,l\}
such that
Z\subset Z_{k}
.

We first claim that
Z
is a monodromically atypical weakly special subvariety of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
. By Proposition
4.11
we know that
Z
is an irreducible component of
\Phi^{-1}(\phi_{i,\Gamma}(\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{P}_{Z},D_{\mathbf{P}_{Z}}),\Gamma_{\mathbf{P}_{Z}}}))
, and by Lemma
5.7
one has

\Phi(Z^{\mathrm{an}})\subset\mathscr{W}_{((\mathbf{P}_{Z},D_{\mathbf{P}_{Z}}),\mathbf{H}_{Z},h_{Z})}\subset\phi_{i,\Gamma}(\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{P}_{Z},D_{\mathbf{P}_{Z}}),\Gamma_{\mathbf{P}_{Z}}})).

This shows that
Z
is an irreducible component of
\Phi^{-1}(\mathscr{W}_{((\mathbf{P}_{Z},D_{\mathbf{P}_{Z}}),\mathbf{H}_{Z},h_{Z})})
hence is a weakly special subvariety. It remains to show that it is monodromically atypical. For this, it suffices to show that

\dim D_{\mathbf{P}_{Z}}-\dim D_{\mathbf{P}}\geqslant\dim q_{\mathbf{H}_{Z},\mathbf{P}_{Z}}^{-1}(h_{Z})-\dim q_{\mathbf{H},\mathbf{P}}^{-1}(h_{S}).

The proof is parallel to
[
9
, Lem. 5.6]
, we recall it briefly. The above inequality is equivalent to

\dim D_{\mathbf{P}_{Z}}/\mathbf{H}_{Z}\geqslant\dim D_{\mathbf{P}}/\mathbf{H}.

Since
\mathbf{H}_{Z}\subset\mathbf{H}
, the inclusion
D_{\mathbf{P}_{Z}}\hookrightarrow D_{\mathbf{P}}
descends to a map
\bar{i}:D_{\mathbf{P}_{Z}}/\mathbf{H}_{Z}\rightarrow D_{\mathbf{P}}/\mathbf{H}
. The image of
\bar{i}
is a Mumford-Tate subdomain of
D_{\mathbf{P}}/\mathbf{H}
which contains
h_{S}=\bar{i}(h_{Z})
. Since
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
is the generic mixed Hodge class of
\mathbb{V}
the point
h_{S}
cannot be contained in a proper Mumford-Tate subdomain of
D_{\mathbf{P}}/\mathbf{H}
. Therefore
\bar{i}
is a surjection and the claimed dimension inequality follows.

Since
Z
is a monodromically atypical weakly special subvariety of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
, it is contained in a maximal one, say
Y
, and by the Geometric Zilber-Pink Theorem
5.11
there exists
((\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}}),\mathbf{N})\in\Sigma_{(S,\mathbb{V})}
such that
\mathbf{H}_{Y}=\mathbf{N}
. We claim that
((\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}}),\mathbf{N})
actually belongs to
\Sigma_{(S,\mathbb{V})}^{\mathrm{sp}}
. Indeed, if
((\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}}),\mathbf{N})\in\Sigma_{(S,\mathbb{V})}^{\mathrm{gen}}
the fact that
\mathbf{H}_{Y}=\mathbf{N}
forces
\dim\Phi_{/\mathbf{N}}(Y^{\mathrm{an}})=0.
Since
Z\subset Y
, this implies that
\dim\Phi_{/\mathbf{N}}(Z^{\mathrm{an}})=0
which contradicts the assumption that
Z
has
\mathbb{V}
-factorwise positive dimension. Unwrapping definitions, this in turn implies that
Y
hence
Z
is contained in
Z_{k}
for some
k\in\{1,\cdots,l\}
, as desired. The non-density statement follows immediately.
∎

A purely unipotent example

In this section, we investigate in a concrete example what can be reasonably expected regarding the distribution of transverse Hodge loci, beyond the pure case. We start by recalling the situation in the pure case. In
[
37
]
we introduced a condition called factorwise
\mathbb{V}
-admissibility on any strict Hodge sub-datum
(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}})
of the generic Hodge datum
(\mathbf{P},D)
of an integral variation of Hodge structure
\mathbb{V}
on a smooth and irreducible quasi-projective complex variety
S
. In substance, we proved there that the transverse Hodge locus of type
\mathbf{M}
of
S
for
\mathbb{V}
is dense in
S^{\mathrm{an}}
for the metric topology if and only if
(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}})
is factorwise
\mathbb{V}
-admissible. The factorwise
\mathbb{V}
-admissibility condition has a natural generalization to the mixed case, which will be shown in Sect. 8 to be necessary for the transverse Hodge locus of prescribed type to be non-empty. This generalization is as follows:

Definition 6.1
.

Let
\mathbb{V}
be an admissible integral graded-polarized variation of mixed Hodge structures on a smooth and irreducible quasi-projective complex algebraic variety
S
. Let
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
be its generic mixed Hodge class and let
\tilde{\Phi}:\widetilde{S^{\mathrm{an}}}\rightarrow D_{\mathbf{P}}
be the lifted period map at some
\tilde{s}\in\widetilde{S^{\mathrm{an}}}
. A strict mixed Hodge subclass
(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}})
of
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
is said
\mathbb{V}
-likely
if for every
\mathbf{N}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{P})
the following inequality holds

\dim q_{\mathbf{N}}(D_{\mathbf{M}})+\dim(q_{\mathbf{N}}\circ\tilde{\Phi})(\widetilde{S^{\mathrm{an}}})-\dim D_{\mathbf{P}}/\mathbf{N}\geqslant 0,

(6.0.1)

where
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{P})
is the set of normal subgroups of
\mathbf{P}
whose radical is unipotent, and for
\mathbf{N}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{P})
we denoted by
q_{\mathbf{N}}:D_{\mathbf{P}}\rightarrow D_{\mathbf{P}}/\mathbf{N}
the quotient.

As demonstrated in a concrete example in the present section, the situation is different and somewhat more complicated in the mixed case than it is in the pure case recalled above. More precisely, we prove:

Proposition 6.2
.

There exists a smooth and irreducible quasi-projective complex curve
C
and an admissible integral graded-polarized variation of mixed Hodge structures
\mathbb{V}
on
C
with generic mixed Hodge class
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
such that:

(i)

There exists a
\mathbb{V}
-likely strict mixed Hodge subclass
(\mathbf{M},D_{M})
of
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
and for any such mixed Hodge subclass:

(
i_{1})

the transverse Hodge locus
\mathrm{HL}(C,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes},\mathbf{M})_{\mathrm{trans}}
of type
\mathbf{M}
is dense in
C(\mathbb{C})
for the Zariski topology;

(i_{2})

the transverse Hodge locus
\mathrm{HL}(C,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes},\mathbf{M})_{\mathrm{trans}}
of type
\mathbf{M}
is not dense in
C(\mathbb{C})
for the metric topology;

(ii)

The full transverse Hodge locus
\mathrm{HL}(C,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes})_{\mathrm{trans}}
is dense in
C(\mathbb{C})
for the metric topology.

This shows that in general the transverse Hodge locus of prescribed type might not be dense in the base for the metric topology even if it is non-empty. However, this leaves open the eventuality that the full transverse Hodge locus is either empty or dense in the base for the metric topology. We also provide an example showing that this weaker statement does not hold either in general:

Proposition 6.3
.

There exists a smooth and irreducible quasi-projective complex curve
C
and an admissible integral graded-polarized variation of mixed Hodge structures
\mathbb{V}
on
C
such that

(i)

\mathrm{HL}(C,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes})_{\mathrm{trans}}
is dense in
C(\mathbb{C})
for the Zariski topology;

(ii)

\mathrm{HL}(C,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes})_{\mathrm{trans}}
is not dense in
C(\mathbb{C})
for the metric topology;

The examples are constructed on a curve in
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}
for some integer
n\geqslant 1
, and to that end, the subsections
6.1-6.3
are dedicated to recollections on the properties of natural variations of mixed Hodge structures on subvarieties of
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}
(for an integer
n\geqslant 1
), and the description of their Hodge locus.

Before starting, we make a notational remark. In what follows, the notation
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
will be used both to denote the complex algebraic variety
\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[x,y]/\braket{xy-1})
(without its natural group structure) and the
\mathbb{C}
-points of the latter endowed with the natural Archimedean topology. We prefer reserving the notation
\mathbb{G}_{m}
for the multiplicative group, seen as a
\mathbb{Q}
-algebraic group. We hope this won’t create any confusion.

The Hodge variety
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}

Fix an integer
n\geqslant 1
. We start by recalling following
[
22
]
that
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}
is canonically isomorphic to
\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathbb{Z}-\mathrm{MHS}}(\mathbb{Z}(0),\mathbb{Z}(1)^{n})
where for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we recall that
\mathbb{Z}(k)
is the Tate Hodge structure, pure of type
(-k,-k)
with underlying abelian group
(2\pi i)^{k}\mathbb{Z}
. In the language of the present paper, this realizes
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}
as a connected Hodge variety. Let us give some detail on this realization.

Let
V_{\mathbb{Z}}=\Big(\bigoplus_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}(2\pi i)\mathbb{Z}\Big)\oplus\mathbb{Z}\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}=V_{\mathbb{C}}
, and set
e_{-2}^{(1)}=(1,0,\cdots,0)
,
e_{-2}^{(2)}=(0,1,\cdots,0)
,
\cdots
,
e_{-2}^{(n)}=(0,0,\cdots,1,0)
and
e_{0}=(0,0,\cdots,0,1)
. These form a
\mathbb{C}
-basis of
V_{\mathbb{C}}
. Furthermore the family
(2\pi ie_{-2}^{(1)},\cdots 2\pi ie_{-2}^{(n)},e_{0})
forms a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
V_{\mathbb{Z}}
. Define a finite ascending filtration on
V_{\mathbb{Q}}
by

W_{-3}V_{\mathbb{Q}}=0\subsetneq W_{-2}V_{\mathbb{Q}}=\bigoplus_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}(2\pi i)\mathbb{Q}\cdot e_{-2}^{(k)}=W_{-1}V_{\mathbb{Q}}\subsetneq W_{0}V_{\mathbb{Q}}=V_{\mathbb{Q}},

and set
h^{-1,-1}=n
,
h^{0,0}=1
and
h^{p,q}=0
for
(p,q)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}-\{(-1,-1),(0,0)\}
. Let
q_{-2}
(resp.
q_{0}
) be the positive definite symmetric bilinear form on
\mathrm{Gr}^{W}_{-2}V_{\mathbb{Q}}\cong\bigoplus_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}(2\pi i)\mathbb{Q}\cdot e_{-2}^{(k)}
(resp. on
\mathrm{Gr}^{W}_{0}V_{\mathbb{Q}}\cong\mathbb{Q}\cdot e_{0}
) with orthonormal basis
(2\pi ie_{-2}^{(1)},\cdots,2\pi ie_{-2}^{(n)})
(resp.
e_{0}
). Let
D_{\mathbf{P}}
be the mixed period domain parametrizing
\mathbb{Q}
-Hodge structures on
V_{\mathbb{Q}}
with weight filtration
W_{\bullet}
, graded-polarized by
(q_{-2},q_{0})
and with Hodge numbers
(h^{p,q})
.

For
\underline{z}=(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
, define

F_{\underline{z}}^{-1}V_{\mathbb{C}}=V_{\mathbb{C}}\supsetneq F_{\underline{z}}^{0}V_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathbb{C}\cdot\Big(e_{0}+\sum_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}z_{k}e^{(k)}_{-2}\Big)\supsetneq F_{\underline{z}}^{1}V_{\mathbb{C}}=0.

Lemma 6.4
.

The map
\psi:\mathbb{C}^{n}\rightarrow D_{\mathbf{P}}
which sends
\underline{z}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
to
(V_{\mathbb{Q}},W_{\bullet},F_{\underline{z}}^{\bullet})
is a biholomorphism.

Proof.

First note that this map is well defined as for any
\underline{z}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
the mixed Hodge structure
\psi(\underline{z})
has by construction the right weight filtration and Hodge numbers. Two different
\underline{z}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
clearly give different
F^{0}
so the map is injective. Conversely, any mixed Hodge structure on
V_{\mathbb{Z}}
with weight filtration and Hodge numbers as above is fully determined by the zeroth piece of its Hodge filtration which is forced to be of rank
1
, so that it must arise as in the previous construction for some
\underline{z}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Thus
\psi
is a bijection between
\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
D_{\mathbf{P}}
. Finally, the formulae show that the Hodge filtration varies holomorphically with
\underline{z}
and unicity in Proposition
2.24
(b) ensures that
\psi
is a biholomorphism.
∎

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
\underline{z}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
. The associated mixed Hodge structure
\psi(\underline{z})
on
V_{\mathbb{Q}}
has only one possible bigrading which is explicitly given by:

V_{\underline{z}}^{-1,-1}=(W_{-2}V_{\mathbb{Q}})_{\mathbb{C}}=\bigoplus_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}\mathbb{C}\cdot e^{(k)}_{-2}\hskip 2.84544pt\mathrm{and}\hskip 2.84544ptV_{\underline{z}}^{0,0}=F_{\underline{z}}^{0}V_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathbb{C}\cdot\Big(e_{0}+\sum_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}z_{k}e^{(k)}_{-2}\Big).

Proof.

Let
\underline{z}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
V_{\mathbb{C}}=\bigoplus_{p,q\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{\underline{z}}^{p,q}
be a bigrading of
\psi(\underline{z})
. Since the only non-zero Hodge numbers are
h^{-1,-1}
and
h^{0,0}
, for
(p,q)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}-\{(-1,-1),(0,0)\}
one has
V_{\underline{z}}^{p,q}=0
which forces:

(W_{-2}V_{\mathbb{Q}})_{\mathbb{C}}=\bigoplus_{p+q\leqslant-2}V_{\underline{z}}^{p,q}=V_{\underline{z}}^{-1,-1},

and

F_{\underline{z}}^{0}V_{\mathbb{C}}=\bigoplus_{p\geqslant 0}V_{\underline{z}}^{p,q}=V_{\underline{z}}^{0,0}.

∎

In the remaining of the section, we set
\mathbf{P}=(\mathbb{G}_{a})^{n}\rtimes\mathbb{G}_{m}
which we see as a
\mathbb{Q}
-algebraic subgroup of
\mathbf{GL}(V_{\mathbb{Q}})
through the following inclusion (where we use the basis
(2\pi i\cdot e_{-2}^{(1)},\cdots,2\pi i\cdot e_{-2}^{(n)},e_{0})
of
V_{\mathbb{Q}}
to write elements of
\mathbf{GL}(V_{\mathbb{Q}})
in matrix form):

(\mathbb{G}_{a})^{n}\rtimes\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\mathbf{GL}(V_{\mathbb{Q}}),(\underline{\xi}=(\xi_{1},\cdots\xi_{n}),\xi)\mapsto M(\underline{\xi},\xi):=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\xi&\cdots&0&\xi_{1}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&\xi&\xi_{n}\\
0&0&0&1\end{array}\right),

where the upper left block in
M(\underline{\xi},\xi)
is
\xi\cdot I_{n}
with
I_{n}
the
n\times n
identity matrix. We denote by
\mathbf{U}=(\mathbb{G}_{a})^{n}
the unipotent radical of
\mathbf{P}
.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
\underline{z}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
and let
\mathbf{P}(\underline{z})
be the Mumford-Tate group of the
\mathbb{Q}
-mixed Hodge structure
\psi(\underline{z})
, seen as a subgroup of
\mathbf{GL}(V_{\mathbb{Q}})
. Then
\mathbf{P}(\underline{z})\subset\mathbf{P}
.

Proof.

Let
\underline{z}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Writing elements of
\mathbf{GL}(V_{\mathbb{C}})
in matrix form for the basis
(2\pi i\cdot e_{-2}^{(1)},\cdots,2\pi i\cdot e_{-2}^{(n)},e_{0})
, the bigrading of
\psi(\underline{z})
exhibited in Lemma
6.5
corresponds to the morphism

x_{\underline{z}}:\mathbb{S}_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow\mathbf{GL}(V_{\mathbb{C}}),(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\mapsto M\Big(\Big(z_{1}\Big(1-\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}\Big),\cdots,z_{n}\Big(1-\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}\Big)\Big),\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}\Big).

Because, by Lemma
6.5
,
\psi(\underline{z})
admits only one bigrading
x_{\underline{z}}
must be the Deligne bigrading of
\psi(\underline{z})
. The explicit description above shows that
x_{\underline{z}}(\mathbb{S}_{\mathbb{C}})
is contained in
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbb{C}}
and Proposition
2.21
then shows that
\mathbf{P}(\underline{z})
is contained in
\mathbf{P}
.
∎

Define a left action of
\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{C})
on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
by setting that
g:=(\underline{\xi},\xi)
acts on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
by

g\cdot\underline{z}=(\xi z_{1}+\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi z_{n}+\xi_{n}).

One easily checks that this indeed defines a group action by biholomorphisms. On the other side, set
\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{C})
to act on
D_{\mathbf{P}}
by conjugation on the target of the (unique) bigrading associated to a point of
D_{\mathbf{P}}
.

Lemma 6.7
.

The map
\psi
is
\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{C})
-equivariant for the above defined actions, and
\mathbf{U}(\mathbb{C})
acts transitively on both.

Proof.

Let
\underline{z}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
g=(\underline{\xi},\xi)\in\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{C})
. One has to check that:

gx_{\underline{z}}g^{-1}=x_{(\xi z_{1}+\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi z_{n}+\xi_{n})}.

This follows from a direct matrix computation which we omit. The transitivity assertion is obvious from the above formula.
∎

Let
\Gamma=\mathbb{G}_{a}^{n}(\mathbb{Z})\subset\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{Q})
which is a neat arithmetic subgroup of
\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{Q})
. For the above action
\Gamma
acts on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
by additive translation, and the map
e=(e^{2\pi i\cdot},\cdots,e^{2\pi i\cdot}):\mathbb{C}^{n}\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}
identifies
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}
to the quotient
\Gamma\backslash\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Since
\Gamma
is neat, it acts on
D_{\mathbf{P}}
properly discontinuously and freely. Therefore, Lemma
6.7
shows that the map
\psi
descends to a biholomorphism

\bar{\psi}:(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}\rightarrow\Gamma\backslash D_{\mathbf{P}}.

This is the claimed realization of
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}
as a (connected) Hodge variety.

Families of extensions of
\mathbb{Z}(0)
by
\mathbb{Z}(1)^{n}

Fix an integer
n\geqslant 1
. Let
S
be a smooth, irreducible and quasi-projective complex algebraic variety, and denote by
(\overline{S},E)
a log-smooth projective compactification of
S
. Let
q=(q_{1},\cdots,q_{n}):S^{\mathrm{an}}\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}
be a holomorphic map. We explain in this subsection how to construct an admissible and graded-polarized integral variation of mixed Hodge structures on
S
from the previous data. We follow closely the exposition of
[
26
]
.

Let
\mathcal{V}=\bigoplus_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}\mathcal{O}_{S^{\mathrm{an}}}\cdot e_{-2}^{(k)}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{S^{\mathrm{an}}}\cdot e_{0}
be a free
\mathcal{O}_{S^{\mathrm{an}}}
-module of rank
n+1
on
S
, endowed with the integrable connection

\nabla:=d-\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}0&\cdots&0&\frac{dq_{1}}{q_{1}}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\frac{dq_{n}}{q_{n}}\\
0&0&0&0\end{array}\right).

For any base point
s\in S^{\mathrm{an}}
, denote by

(m_{1}(q,\cdot),\cdots,m_{n}(q,\cdot)):\pi_{1}(S^{\mathrm{an}},s)\rightarrow\pi_{1}((\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n},q(s))\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}

the map induced by
q
at the level of fundamental groups.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
s\in S^{\mathrm{an}}
. The monodromy representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(S^{\mathrm{an}},s)\rightarrow\mathbf{GL}(\mathcal{V}_{s})
associated to
(\mathcal{V},\nabla)
at the basepoint
s
is given, in the basis
(e_{-2}^{(1)},\cdots,e_{-2}^{(n)},e_{0})
, by the formula:

\forall\gamma\in\pi_{1}(S^{\mathrm{an}},s),\rho(\gamma)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}1&\cdots&0&(2\pi i)m_{1}(q,\gamma)\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&1&(2\pi i)m_{n}(q,\gamma)\\
0&0&0&1\end{array}\right).

Proof.

Let
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow S^{\mathrm{an}}
be a continuous loop. The horizontal sections of
(\mathcal{V},\nabla)
along the loop
\gamma
are linear combinations of
e_{-2}^{(1)},\cdots,e_{-2}^{(n)}
and

t\in[0,1]\mapsto e_{0}+\sum_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}\log(q_{k}\circ\gamma(t))e_{-2}^{(k)}

for some local determination of the logarithm. The result follows immediately.
∎

This endows the local system of solutions to
(\mathcal{V},\nabla)
with an integral structure
\mathbb{V}
given on the fiber over
s\in S^{\mathrm{an}}
by

\mathbb{V}_{s}=\Big(\bigoplus_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}(2\pi i)\mathbb{Z}e_{-2}^{(k)}\Big)\oplus\mathbb{Z}(e_{0}+\sum_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}\log(q_{1}(z))e_{-2}^{(k)})\subset\mathcal{V}_{s}

for some determination of the logarithm (note that the result is independent of this choice). We have associated to the datum
(C,q)
an integral local system which we now endow with a variation of mixed Hodge structures.

Consider the following filtrations by
\mathcal{O}_{S^{\mathrm{an}}}
-submodules

\mathcal{W}_{-3}=0\subsetneq\mathcal{W}_{-2}=\bigoplus_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}\mathcal{O}_{S^{\mathrm{an}}}\cdot e_{-2}^{(k)}=\mathcal{W}_{-1}\subsetneq\mathcal{W}_{0}=\mathcal{V},

and

\mathcal{F}^{-1}=\mathcal{V}\supsetneq\mathcal{F}^{0}=\mathcal{O}_{S^{\mathrm{an}}}\cdot e_{0}\supsetneq\mathcal{F}^{1}=0.

The following identities are immediate from the construction

\nabla(\mathcal{W}_{k})\subseteq\mathcal{W}_{k}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{C}.

\nabla(\mathcal{F}^{k})\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{k-1}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{C}

The first one says that the filtration
\mathcal{W}_{\bullet}
is by flat subbundles, and it is easily seen to give a filtration of
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbb{Q}}
by integral sub-local systems
\mathbb{W}_{\bullet}
. We call it the "weight filtration". The second identity is Griffiths transversality for
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
. We call the latter filtration the "Hodge filtration". The following is immediate:

Lemma 6.9
.

The fitrations
(\mathbb{W}_{\bullet},\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})
endow
\mathbb{V}
with the structure of an integral and graded-polarized variation of mixed Hodge structures on
S
, which is an extension of
\underline{\mathbb{Z}(0)}
by
\underline{\mathbb{Z}(1)}^{n}
. The associated period map identifies naturally to
\bar{\psi}^{-1}\circ q
.

It remains to study the behavior near the points of
E
. This is the purpose of the following.

Proposition 6.10
.

Assume that for any holomorphic map
i:\Delta\rightarrow\overline{S}^{\mathrm{an}}
such that
i(\Delta^{\times})\subset S^{\mathrm{an}}
, the composite
q\circ i
is meromorphic at
0
. Then, the associated variation
(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{W}_{\bullet},\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})
is admissible.

Proof.

Let
i:\Delta\rightarrow\overline{S}^{\mathrm{an}}
be a holomorphic map such that
i(\Delta^{\times})\subset S
. We need to prove that
i^{\ast}(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{W}_{\bullet},\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})
is admissible. We make the usual identification
\pi_{1}(\Delta^{\times})\cong\mathbb{Z}
, and denote by
\rho_{i}
the monodromy representation associated to
i^{\ast}\mathbb{V}
. According to Lemma
6.8
, there exists a
n
-uple of integers
(m_{1},\cdots,m_{n})
such that in the basis
(e_{-2}^{(1)},\cdots,e_{-2}^{(n)},e_{0})
, one has:

T:=\rho_{i}(1)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}1&\cdots&0&(2\pi i)m_{1}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&1&(2\pi i)m_{n}\\
0&0&0&1\end{array}\right).

Clearly this shows that the monodromy is unipotent, and actually
(T-1)^{2}=0
so that
N:=\log(T)=T-1
. Besides, since the monodromy is trivial on the graded pieces, we are in the setting of the following:

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
V
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space endowed with a finite increasing filtration
W_{\bullet}
and a nilpotent endomorphism
N
which preserves
W
. Assume that the induced endomorphism
\mathrm{Gr}^{W}(N):\mathrm{Gr}^{W}(V)\rightarrow\mathrm{Gr}^{W}(V)
is zero. Then
V
admits a weight filtration for
N
relative to
W_{\bullet}
if and only if for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}

N(W_{k})\subset W_{k-2}.

Let
z\in\Delta^{\times}
. We have
N(e_{0})=e_{0}+\sum_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}m_{k}e_{-2}^{(k)}\in(\mathbb{W}_{-2})_{z}
. Therefore the lemma shows that
\mathbb{V}_{z}
admits a weight filtration for
N
relative to
(\mathbb{W}_{\bullet})_{z}
.

It remains to establish the extension property. We first recall the construction of Deligne’s canonical extension. Consider the connection on
\mathcal{V}
defined by

D=d-\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}0&\cdots&0&m_{1}\frac{dz}{z}-\frac{dq_{1}}{q_{1}}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&m_{n}\frac{dz}{z}-\frac{dq_{n}}{q_{n}}\\
0&0&0&0\end{array}\right).

The pair
(\mathcal{V},D)
is easily seen to have trivial associated monodromy representation, and the
D
-horizontal sections of
\mathcal{V}
are well defined on the whole punctured disk and give another trivialization of
\mathcal{V}
:

\mathcal{V}=\Big(\bigoplus_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta^{\times}}\cdot e_{-2}^{(k)}\Big)\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\Delta^{\times}}\cdot\tilde{e_{0}}

where
\tilde{e_{0}}=e_{0}+\sum_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}\log(\frac{q_{k}(i(z))}{z^{m_{k}}})e_{-2}^{(k)}
.
The canonical extension of
\mathcal{V}
to
\Delta
is then defined as the extension as a constant bundle for the above trivialization:

\mathcal{V}=\Big(\bigoplus_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\cdot e_{-2}^{(k)}\Big)\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\cdot\tilde{e_{0}}

By assumption, the map
{}^{i}q:=q\circ(i|_{\Delta^{\times}})
is meromorphic at
0
i.e. its components
(^{i}q_{k})_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}
are meromorphic at
0
as functions
\Delta^{\times}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. For
k\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
, there is by definition a unique writing

{}^{i}q_{k}(z)=z^{l_{k}}(a_{k}+f_{k}(z))

where
l_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}
,
a_{k}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
and
f_{k}:\Delta\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is a holomorphic map vanishing at
0
.

Lemma 6.12
.

For
k\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
, one has
l_{k}=n_{k}
.

Proof.

Let
k\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
. Consider a continuous loop
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\Delta^{\times}
whose homotopy class is the generator
1
of
\pi_{1}(\Delta^{\times})
. By definition
n_{k}
is the index of
{}^{i}\gamma_{k}:=^{i}q_{k}\circ\gamma
around
0
. If
l_{k}=0
, one therefore finds

2\pi in_{k}=\int_{{}^{i}\gamma_{k}}\frac{dz}{z}=\int_{\gamma}\frac{q^{\prime}(u)}{q(u)}du=\int_{\gamma}\frac{f^{\prime}_{k}(u)}{a_{k}+f_{k}(u)}du

which is zero by the theorem of residues since the integrand of the last integral is holomorphic on the disk. Since we assumed
l_{k}=0
, this proves that
n_{k}=l_{k}
. If
l_{k}\neq 0
, the same formula gives

2\pi in_{k}=\int_{{}^{i}\gamma_{k}}\frac{dz}{z}=\int_{\gamma}\frac{q^{\prime}(u)}{q(u)}du=\int_{\gamma}\frac{l_{k}du}{u}du+\int_{\gamma}\frac{f^{\prime}_{k}(u)}{a_{k}+f_{k}(u)}du

The second integral in the right hand side is zero as explained in the
l_{k}=0
case. The first integral in the right hand side is
2\pi il_{k}
times the the index of
\gamma
which is
1
by definition. This finishes the proof.
∎

It follows from the above Lemma
6.12
that for
k\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
, one has for
z\in\Delta^{\times}
:

\log(\frac{{}^{i}q_{k}(z)}{z^{n_{k}}})=\log(a_{k}+f_{k}(z))

which has a finite limit as
z\rightarrow 0
. Define the extension with the following formula, which makes sense by what precedes:

\overline{\mathcal{F}^{0}}=\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\cdot\Big(\tilde{e_{0}}-\sum_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}\log(\frac{q_{k}(i(z))}{z^{n_{k}}})e_{-2}^{(k)}\Big)

This defines a locally free
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}
-submodule of
\overline{\mathcal{V}}
, which is everywhere a supplement of

\overline{\mathcal{W}_{-2}}=\bigoplus_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\cdot e_{-2}^{(k)}.

This is what we wanted to prove.
∎

Special subvarieties of
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}
: torus versus mixed period domain

Fix an integer
n\geqslant 1
. The purpose of this subsection is to prove the equivalence between two notions of "special subvarieties" of
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}
and to use this to give a manageable description of the Hodge locus of the variations of mixed Hodge structures of the type described in the previous subsection. Special subvarieties will always be considered to be proper subvarieties (i.e. we remove
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}
from the list).

We will call
t
-special
a special subvariety of
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}
seen as a torus. Recall that
t
-special subvariety of
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}
are translates of subtori of
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}
by torsion points, that is subvarieties of the form
\underline{\zeta}\cdot T(\mathbb{C})
, where
\underline{\zeta}=(e^{2\pi i\alpha_{j}})_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n}
(with
(\alpha_{j})\in\mathbb{Q}^{n}
) acts by component-wise multiplication on
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}
, and
T
is a
\mathbb{C}
-algebraic subtorus of
\mathbb{G}_{m,\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. Following
[
13
, (3.2.6)]
, a subgroup
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{n}
is called
primitive
if
\Lambda=(\Lambda\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R})\cap\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. By
[
13
, Thm. 3.2.19]
, there is a bijection between subgroups of
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and algebraic subgroups of
\mathbb{G}_{m,\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. By
[
13
, Cor. 3.2.8]
, primitive subgroups of
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
correspond under this bijection to algebraic subtori of
\mathbb{G}_{m,\mathbb{C}}^{n}
. Explicitely, this bijection associates to
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{n}
the algebraic subgroup of
\mathbb{G}_{m,\mathbb{C}}^{n}
with
\mathbb{C}
-points

T_{\Lambda}(\mathbb{C})=\Big\{\underline{x}=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\in(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}\hskip 2.84544pt:\hskip 2.84544pt\forall\underline{\lambda}=(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{n})\in\Lambda,\prod_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}x_{k}^{\lambda_{k}}=1\Big\}

An
h
-special subvariety
of
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}
is defined to be a subvariety of the form

\bar{\psi}^{-1}(\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}}),\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}})

where
\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}}),\Gamma_{\mathbf{M}}}
is a mixed Hodge subvariety of
\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}}),\Gamma}
. Recall that a mixed Hodge subvariety of
\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}}),\Gamma}
is a subvariety of the form
\pi_{\Gamma}(D_{\mathbf{M}})
for some mixed Hodge subclass
(\mathbf{M},D_{M})
of
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
, where
\pi_{\Gamma}:D_{\mathbf{P}}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}}),\Gamma}
is the quotient map. Therefore, we are tasked with classifying strict mixed Hodge subclasses of
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
.

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
(\mathbf{M},D_{M})
be a strict mixed Hodge subclass of
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
. There exists a strict (unipotent)
\mathbb{Q}
-algebraic subgroup
\mathbf{M}^{u}
of
\mathbf{P}
contained in
\mathbf{U}=(\mathbb{G}_{a})^{n}
and normalized by
\mathbb{G}_{m}
, and a
g\in\mathbf{U}(\mathbb{Q})
such that:

\mathbf{M}=g(\mathbf{M}^{u}\rtimes\mathbb{G}_{m})g^{-1}.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{M}^{u}
be the unipotent radical of
\mathbf{M}
. By assumption, there exists some
\psi(\underline{z})\in D_{\mathbf{P}}
whose bigraduation
x_{\underline{z}}
factors through
\mathbf{M}_{\mathbb{C}}
, and from the formula for
x_{\underline{z}}
one sees that
\bar{x}_{\underline{z}}:\mathbb{S}_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow(\mathbf{M}/\mathbf{M}^{u})_{\mathbb{C}}
has positive dimensional image. Consider a Levi decomposition
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{M}^{u}\rtimes\mathbf{G}
. By the previous remark, one has
\dim\mathbf{G}>0
. As
\mathbf{G}
is a closed reductive
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup of
\mathbf{P}
,
[
15
, Prop. 5.1]
ensures the existence of a
g\in\mathbf{U}(\mathbb{Q})
such that
g^{-1}\mathbf{G}g\subset\mathbb{G}_{m}
. Because
\dim\mathbf{G}>0
, this forces
g^{-1}\mathbf{G}g=\mathbb{G}_{m}
as subgroups of
\mathbf{P}
, i.e.
\mathbf{G}=g\mathbb{G}_{m}g^{-1}
. In particular,
\mathbf{M}^{u}
is normalized by
g\mathbb{G}_{m}g^{-1}
. Finally, since the group
\mathbf{U}
is commutative and
g\in\mathbf{U}(\mathbb{Q})
, we have that
\mathbf{M}^{u}
is also normalized by
\mathbb{G}_{m}
and that
\mathbf{M}^{u}\rtimes g\mathbb{G}_{m}g^{-1}=g(\mathbf{M}^{u}\rtimes\mathbb{G}_{m})g^{-1}
. This proves the statement.
∎

We can now turn to listing strict mixed Hodge subclasses of
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
. Let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be a primitive subgroup. We define

D_{\Lambda}=\psi\Big(\Big\{(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\hskip 2.84544pt:\hskip 2.84544pt\forall\underline{\lambda}=(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{n})\in\Lambda,\sum_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}\lambda_{k}z_{k}=0\Big\}\Big)\subset D_{\mathbf{P}}.

Consider the
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup
\mathbf{U}_{\Lambda}
of
\mathbf{U}
whose
R
-points, for any
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
R
, are

\mathbf{U}_{\Lambda}(R)=\Big\{M(\underline{\xi},1):\underline{\xi}=(\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{n})\in R^{n},\forall\underline{\lambda}=(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{n})\in\Lambda,\sum_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}\lambda_{k}\xi_{k}=0\Big\},

and
\mathbf{M}_{\Lambda}=\mathbf{U}_{\Lambda}\rtimes\mathbb{G}_{m}
whose
R
-points, for any
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
R
, are

\mathbf{M}_{\Lambda}(R)=\Big\{M(\underline{\xi},\xi):\xi\in R^{\times},\underline{\xi}=(\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{n})\in R^{n},\forall\underline{\lambda}=(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{n})\in\Lambda,\sum_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}\lambda_{k}\xi_{k}=0\Big\}.

One easily checks that these indeed define connected
\mathbb{Q}
-algebraic subgroups of
\mathbf{U}
and
\mathbf{P}
respectively. Finally, for
g=(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n})\in\mathbf{U}(\mathbb{Q})=\mathbb{Q}^{n}
, set

g\cdot D_{\Lambda}=\psi\Big(\Big\{(z_{1}+\alpha_{1},\cdots,z_{n}+\alpha_{n})\hskip 2.84544pt:\hskip 2.84544pt(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n},\psi(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})\in D_{\Lambda}\Big\}\Big)\subset D_{\mathbf{P}}.

the translate of
D_{\Lambda}
by
g
for the
\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{C})
-action on
D_{\mathbf{P}}
defined previously.

Proposition 6.14
.

The map which associates
(g\mathbf{M}_{\Lambda}g^{-1},g\cdot D_{\Lambda})
to a pair
(\Lambda,g)
is a bijection between

•

the set of pairs
(\Lambda,g)
where
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{n}
is a primitive subgroup and
g\in\mathbf{U}(\mathbb{Q})
;

•

the set of strict mixed Hodge subclasses of
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
.

Proof.

Step
1
: The pairs
(g\mathbf{M}_{\Lambda}g^{-1},g\cdot D_{\Lambda})
are indeed mixed Hodge subclasses of
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
.

It suffices to prove this statement for
g=1
because the conjugate of a strict mixed Hodge subclass of
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
by a rational element
g\in\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{Q})
is still a strict mixed Hodge subclass of
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
. Let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be a primitive subgroup. We want to prove that
D_{\Lambda}
is the
\mathbf{M}_{\Lambda}(\mathbb{R})\mathbf{U}_{\Lambda}(\mathbb{C})
-orbit of a point in
D_{\mathbf{P}}
whose associated canonical bigraduation factors through
(\mathbf{M}_{\Lambda})_{\mathbb{C}}
. Let
\psi(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})\in D_{\Lambda}
. The associated canonical bigraduation is

x_{\underline{z}}:\mathbb{S}_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow\mathbf{GL}(V_{\mathbb{C}}),(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\mapsto M\Big(\big(z_{1}\big(1-\frac{1}{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\big),\cdots,z_{n}\big(1-\frac{1}{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\big)\big),\frac{1}{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\Big).

whose image clearly lies in
(\mathbf{M}_{\Lambda})_{\mathbb{C}}
. Furthermore, given an element
\mathbf{M}_{\Lambda}(\mathbb{R})\mathbf{U}_{\Lambda}(\mathbb{C})
seen as a matrix
M(\underline{\xi},\xi)
with
\underline{\xi}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
and
\sum_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}\lambda_{k}\xi_{k}=0
for any
\underline{\lambda}=(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{n})\in\Lambda
, one has for
\psi(\underline{z})\in D_{\Lambda}
:

M(\underline{\xi},\xi)\cdot\psi(\underline{z})=\psi(\underline{\xi}+\xi\underline{z})=\psi(\xi_{1}+\xi z_{1},\cdots,\xi_{n}+\xi z_{n}).

For any
\underline{\lambda}=(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{n})\in\Lambda
, one has:

\sum_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}\lambda_{k}(\xi_{k}+\xi z_{k})=\sum_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}\lambda_{k}\xi_{k}+\xi\sum_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}\lambda_{k}z_{k}=0

since
\psi(\underline{z})\in D_{\Lambda}
and
M(\underline{\xi},\xi)\in\mathbf{M}_{\Lambda}(\mathbb{R})\mathbf{U}_{\Lambda}(\mathbb{C})
. Therefore,
M(\underline{\xi},\xi)\cdot\psi(\underline{z})\in D_{\Lambda}
, and
\mathbf{M}_{\Lambda}(\mathbb{R})\mathbf{U}_{\Lambda}(\mathbb{C})
acts on
D_{\Lambda}
and is easily seen to act transitively from the formula above. This finishes Step
1
.

Step
2
: Any strict mixed Hodge subclass of
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
is of the form
(g\mathbf{M}_{\Lambda}g^{-1},g\cdot D_{\Lambda})
.

Let
(\mathbf{M},D_{M})
be a strict mixed Hodge subclass of
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
. By Lemma
6.13
, there exists a
g\in\mathbf{U}(\mathbb{Q})
and a strict algebraic
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup
\mathbf{M}^{u}
of
\mathbf{U}
normalized by
\mathbb{G}_{m}
such that

\mathbf{M}=g(\mathbf{M}^{u}\rtimes\mathbb{G}_{m})g^{-1}.

The group
\mathbf{M}^{u}
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-algebraic subgroup of
(\mathbb{G}_{a})^{n}
which a vector group, as an abelian
\mathbb{Q}
-algebraic unipotent group. Hence, the
\mathbb{Q}
-algebraic subgroups of
(\mathbb{G}_{a})^{n}
are in bijection with
\mathbb{Q}
-vector subspaces of
\mathbb{Q}^{n}
(as these are all Lie subalgebras for the trivial bracket). Let
W
be such a
\mathbb{Q}
-vector subspace, and denote by
r
its codimension. Then
W
can be defined in
\mathbb{Q}^{n}
by
r
independent linear equations in
n
variables with rational coefficients. Multiplying each equation by the denominators of its coefficient, we can assume that the equations have integer coefficients. Denote the
j^{\mathrm{th}}
equation
(1\leqslant j\leqslant r)
by

\sum_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}\lambda_{k}^{(j)}z_{k}=0.

Since the equations are independent, the vectors
\lambda^{(j)}:=(\lambda_{1}^{(j)},\cdots,\lambda_{n}^{(j)})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
span a rank
r
subgroup
\Lambda_{0}
of
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. Let
\Lambda=(\Lambda_{0}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R})\cap\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be the corresponding primitive subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. Then, by construction

W=\Big\{(w_{1},\cdots,w_{n})\in\mathbb{Q}^{n}\hskip 2.84544pt:\hskip 2.84544pt\forall\underline{\lambda}=(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{n})\in\Lambda,\sum_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}\lambda_{k}w_{k}=0\Big\}.

It follows that the subgroup of
\mathbf{U}
corresponding to the Lie subalgebra
W
of
\mathrm{Lie}(\mathbf{U})
is
\mathbf{U}_{\Lambda}
. So we have proved that for any mixed Hodge subclass
(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}})
of
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
, there exists some
g\in\mathbf{U}(\mathbb{Q})
and some primitive subgroup
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{n}
such that
\mathbf{M}=g\mathbf{M}_{\Lambda}g^{-1}
. It remains to see that
g\cdot D_{\mathbf{M}}=g\cdot D_{\Lambda}
. This is clear from the proof in Step
1
that
D_{\Lambda}
is a
\mathbf{M}_{\Lambda}(\mathbb{R})\mathbf{U}_{\Lambda}(\mathbb{C})
-orbit and the following chain of equivalences for
\underline{z}=(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
:

\displaystyle x_{\underline{z}}(\mathbb{S}_{\mathbb{C}})\subset(\mathbf{M}_{\Lambda})_{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\forall(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{C}),\forall\underline{\lambda}=(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{n})\in\Lambda,\sum_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}\lambda_{k}z_{k}\big(1-\frac{1}{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\big)=0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\forall\underline{\lambda}=(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{n})\in\Lambda,\sum_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}\lambda_{k}z_{k}=0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\psi(\underline{z})\in D_{\Lambda}.

∎

Corollary 6.15
.

t
-special and
h
-special subvarieties of
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}
coïncide.

Proof.

Let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be a primitive subgroup and
g=(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n})\in\mathbf{U}(\mathbb{Q})=\mathbb{Q}^{n}
. Denote by
\underline{\zeta}
the torsion point
(e^{2i\pi\alpha_{1}},\cdots e^{2i\pi\alpha_{n}})
of
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}
. The result will follow from the fact that
\bar{\psi}(\pi_{\Gamma}(g\cdot D_{\Lambda}))=\underline{\zeta}\cdot T_{\Lambda}(\mathbb{C})
. This follows from an immediate computation, using the fact that under the identifications
D_{\mathbf{P}}\cong\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}}),\Gamma}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}
, the uniformization
\pi_{\Gamma}
identifies to
\mathbb{C}^{n}\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n},(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})\mapsto(e^{2i\pi z_{1}},\cdots,e^{2i\pi z_{2}})
.
∎

Corollary 6.16
.

Let
S
be smooth and irreducible quasi-projective complex algebraic variety and
q:S^{\mathrm{an}}\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}
be a holomorphic map. Let
\mathbb{V}
be the associated integral and graded-polarized variation of mixed Hodge structure on
S
. Then:

(i)

\mathbb{V}
has generic mixed Hodge class equal to
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
if and only if
q(S^{\mathrm{an}})
is not contained in some
\underline{\zeta}\cdot T_{\Lambda}
for some primitive subgroup
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{n}
of positive rank and
\underline{\zeta}=(e^{2\pi i\alpha_{1}},\cdots,e^{2\pi i\alpha_{n}})
with
(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n})\in\mathbb{Q}^{n}
;

(ii)

If
\mathbb{V}
has generic mixed Hodge class equal to
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
,

\mathrm{HL}(S,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes})=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n})\in\mathbb{Q}^{n}\\
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{n}\hskip 2.84544pt\mathrm{primitive}\\
\mathrm{rk}(\Lambda)>0\end{subarray}}q^{-1}\Big((e^{2\pi i\alpha_{1}},\cdots,e^{2\pi i\alpha_{n}})\cdot T_{\Lambda}(\mathbb{C})\Big).

Proof.

Immediate from definitions and the elucidation of mixed Hodge subvarieties of
\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}}),\Gamma}
.
∎

We can readily prove Proposition
6.3
.

Proof of Proposition
6.3
.

Let
C=\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}-\{0\}
and let
\mathbb{V}
be the variation whose period map is the identity
\mathbb{C}^{\times}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. By Corollary
6.16
we find that
\mathrm{HL}(C,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes})_{\mathrm{trans}}=\mu_{\infty}
where
\mu_{\infty}\subset\mathbb{C}^{\times}=C(\mathbb{C})
is the set of roots of unity in
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, which is dense in
C(\mathbb{C})
for the Zariski topology but not for the metric topology.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.2

We reformulate the elucidation made in the previous section of special subvarieties of
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}
in the case where
n=2
. A positive rank subgroup
\Lambda
of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
is either
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
or free of rank
1
. In the former case, the corresponding mixed Hodge subclass of
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
is
(\mathbb{G}_{m},\{(0,0)\})
. In the latter case, if we denote by
(b,-a)
a generator of
\Lambda
(with
(a,b)\neq(0,0)
), one checks that
\Lambda
is primitive if and only if
a
and
b
are coprime. In that case, the corresponding special subvariety is explicitely given by

Y_{a,b}:=T_{\mathbb{Z}\cdot(b,-a)}(\mathbb{C})=\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2}\hskip 2.84544pt:\hskip 2.84544ptx_{1}^{b}=x_{2}^{a}\}=\{(x^{a},x^{b})\hskip 2.84544pt:\hskip 2.84544ptx\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}\}.

We denote by
(\mathbf{M}_{a,b},D_{a,b})=(\mathbf{M}_{\mathbb{Z}\cdot(b,-a)},D_{\mathbb{Z}\cdot(b,-a)})
the corresponding mixed Hodge class, and by
\mathbf{U}_{a,b}=\mathbf{U}_{\mathbb{Z}\cdot(b,-a)}
the unipotent radical of
\mathbf{M}_{a,b}
. With these notations, the classification of Proposition
6.14
reads:

Corollary 6.17
.

The strict mixed Hodge subclasses of
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
are exactly those of the following form:

(i)

(g\mathbb{G}_{m}g^{-1},\{g\})
for some
g=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\mathbb{Q}^{2}
;

(ii)

(g\mathbf{M}_{a,b}g^{-1},g\cdot D_{a,b})
for some
(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}-\{(0,0)\}
and
g=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\mathbb{Q}^{2}
.

Consider the algebraic curve
C
in
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2}
defined in coordinates
(x_{1},x_{2})
by the equation
x_{1}^{2}-3x_{2}=0
. This is a smooth and irreducible algebraic curve, and we denote by
q:C\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2}
the inclusion. By the Section 6.2, this defines an admissible integral graded-polarized variation of mixed Hodge structures
\mathbb{V}
over
S
which is an extension of
\underline{\mathbb{Z}(0)}
by
\underline{\mathbb{Z}(1)}^{2}
and has period map
q
.

Lemma 6.18
.

The generic mixed Hodge class of
\mathbb{V}
is
((\mathbb{G}_{a})^{2}\rtimes\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathbb{C}^{2})
.

Proof.

It suffices, by Corollary
6.16
, to prove that
C
not contained in a subvariety of
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2}
of the form
\zeta\cdot Y_{a,b}
for some
(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}-\{(0,0)\}
and some
\zeta=(e^{2i\pi\alpha_{1}},e^{2i\pi\alpha_{2}})
with
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\mathbb{Q}^{2}
. Let
(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}-\{(0,0)\}
and
\zeta=(e^{2i\pi\alpha_{1}},e^{2i\pi\alpha_{2}})
as above. If
(x_{1},x_{2})\in C\cap\zeta\cdot Y_{a,b}
, one has
|x_{1}|^{2a}=3^{a}|x_{1}|^{b}
. Since
(a,b)\neq(0,0)
, one cannot have
b=2a
because otherwise one would have
3^{a}=1
which implies
a=0
hence
b=0
. Therefore,
|x_{1}|=e^{\frac{a}{2a-b}\log(3)}
. Since,
C
contains points with first coordinate of arbitrary large module, this shows that
C\cap\zeta\cdot Y_{a,b}\subsetneq C
, which is the desired result.
∎

We now give the exhaustive list of
\mathbb{V}
-likely strict mixed Hodge subclasses
(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}})
of
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
. Start with the following:

Lemma 6.19
.

The set
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{P})
is
\{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{2},\{0\}\}\cup\{\mathbf{U}_{a,b}\hskip 2.84544pt:\hskip 2.84544pt(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}-\{(0,0)\}\}
. For
(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}-\{(0,0)\}
, the quotient mixed Hodge class
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})/\mathbf{U}_{a,b}
identifies to
(\mathbb{G}_{a}\rtimes\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathbb{C})
, and under this identification, the quotient map
q_{\mathbf{U}_{a,b}}:D_{\mathbf{P}}\rightarrow D_{\mathbf{P}}/\mathbf{U}_{a,b}
identifies to

q_{\mathbf{U}_{a,b}}:\mathbb{C}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{C},(z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto bz_{1}-az_{2},

and
p_{\mathbf{U}_{a,b}}
identifies to the map

p_{\mathbf{U}_{a,b}}:(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2}\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{\times}),(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto x_{1}^{b}x_{2}^{-a}.

Proof.

The description of
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{P})
is an easy exercise which we leave to the reader. Let
(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}-\{(0,0)\}
. Consider the map
\pi_{\mathbf{U}_{a,b}}:\mathbf{P}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{a}\rtimes\mathbb{G}_{m}
which on
R
-points for any
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
R
maps
((\xi_{1},\xi_{2}),\xi)
to
(b\xi_{1}-a\xi_{2},\xi)
. One easily checks that
\pi_{\mathbf{U}_{a,b}}
is a morphism of
\mathbb{Q}
-algebraic groups whose kernel is exactly
\mathbf{U}_{a,b}
. The claimed description of
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})/\mathbf{U}_{a,b}
,
q_{\mathbf{U}_{a,b}}
and
p_{\mathbf{U}_{a,b}}
follows.
∎

In view of the classification of Corollary
6.17
, the following gives a complete description of
\mathbb{V}
-likely strict mixed Hodge subclasses of
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
.

Lemma 6.20
.

Let
(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}})\subsetneq(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
be a strict mixed Hodge subclass.

(i)

If
(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}})=(g\mathbb{G}_{m}g^{-1},\{g\})
for some
g=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\mathbb{Q}^{2}
, it is not
\mathbb{V}
-likely;

(ii)

If
(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}})=(g\mathbf{M}_{a,b}g^{-1},g\cdot D_{a,b})
for some
g=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\mathbb{G}_{a}^{2}(\mathbb{Q})
and
(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}-\{(0,0)\}
, one has:

(ii_{1})

if
(a,b)=(1,2)
then
(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}})
is not
\mathbb{V}
-likely;

(ii_{2})

otherwise
(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}})
is
\mathbb{V}
-likely.

Proof.

(i)
is immediate. Let
(a,b),(c,d)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}-\{(0,0)\}
and
g=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\mathbb{Q}^{2}
. Then:

p_{\mathbf{U}_{c,d}}(C^{\mathrm{an}})=\{\frac{x^{d-2c}}{3^{c}}\hskip 2.84544pt:\hskip 2.84544ptx\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}\}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\{1\}&\mathrm{if}(c,d)=(1,2)\\
\mathbb{C}^{\times}&\mathrm{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

On the other hand
p_{\mathbf{U}_{c,d}}(g\cdot Y_{a,b})=\mathbb{C}^{\times}
if
(a,b)\neq(c,d)
and equal to
\{e^{2i\pi g}\}
otherwise. Therefore, if
(a,b)=(1,2)
the condition (
6.0.1
) fails for
\mathbf{N}=\mathbf{U}_{1,2}
:

\dim p_{\mathbf{U}_{1,2}}(C^{\mathrm{an}})+\dim p_{\mathbf{U}_{1,2}}(g\cdot Y_{1,2})=0+0<1=\dim D_{\mathbf{P}}/\mathbf{U}_{1,2}.

Therefore
(g\mathbf{M}_{1,2}g^{-1},g\cdot D_{1,2})
is not
\mathbb{V}
-likely. If
(a,b)\neq(1,2)
, one has for
(c,d)\neq(1,2)

\dim p_{\mathbf{U}_{c,d}}(C^{\mathrm{an}})+\dim p_{\mathbf{U}_{c,d}}(g\cdot Y_{a,b})=1+\dim p_{\mathbf{U}_{c,d}}(g\cdot Y_{a,b})\geqslant 1=\dim D_{\mathbf{P}}/\mathbf{U}_{c,d},

and for
(c,d)=(1,2)
:

\dim p_{\mathbf{U}_{1,2}}(C^{\mathrm{an}})+\dim p_{\mathbf{U}_{1,2}}(g\cdot Y_{a,b})=0+1\geqslant 1=\dim D_{\mathbf{P}}/\mathbf{U}_{1,2}.

This proves that for
(a,b)\neq(1,2)
the mixed Hodge subclass
(g\mathbf{M}_{a,b}g^{-1},g\cdot D_{a,b})
of
(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
is
\mathbb{V}
-likely.
∎

Finally, Proposition
6.2
is a consequence of the following.

Proposition 6.21
.

There exists a
\mathbb{V}
-likely strict mixed Hodge subclass
(\mathbf{M},D_{M})\subsetneq(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
and for any such mixed Hodge subclass,

(i)

The transverse Hodge locus
\mathrm{HL}(C,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes},\mathbf{M})_{\mathrm{trans}}
of type
\mathbf{M}
is not dense in
C^{\mathrm{an}}
for the metric topology, but is dense in
C
for the Zariski topology;

(ii)

The Hodge locus
\mathrm{HL}(C,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes})_{\mathrm{trans}}
is dense in
C^{\mathrm{an}}
for the metric topology.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.20
there exists
(\mathbf{M},D_{M})\subsetneq(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
a
\mathbb{V}
-likely strict mixed Hodge subclass and for any such mixed Hodge subclass, there exists
g\in\mathbb{G}_{a}^{2}(\mathbb{Q})
and
(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}-\{(0,0),(1,2)\}
such that
(\mathbf{M},D_{M})=(g\mathbf{M}_{a,b}g^{-1},g\cdot D_{a,b})
.

(i)
First remark that for dimension reasons and by Hodge genericity of
C
in
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2}
, we have

\mathrm{HL}(C,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes},\mathbf{M})=\mathrm{HL}(C,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes},\mathbf{M})_{\mathrm{trans}},

so we are tasked with studying the distribution of
\mathrm{HL}(C,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes},\mathbf{M})
in full. Besides, we have the description:

\mathrm{HL}(C,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes},\mathbf{M})=\bigcup_{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\mathbb{Q}^{2}}C\cap(e^{2\pi i\alpha_{1}},e^{2\pi i\alpha_{2}})\cdot Y_{a,b}.

Proceding as in the proof on Lemma
6.18
, we find that for any
(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathrm{HL}(C,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes},\mathbf{M})
, one has
|x_{1}|=e^{\frac{a}{2a-b}\log(3)}
which readily proves that
\mathrm{HL}(C,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes},\mathbf{M})
is not dense in
C
for the metric topology.

Since
a
and
b
are coprime and
(a,b)\neq(1,2)
, one has
b\neq 2a
. To prove Zariski density of
\mathrm{HL}(C,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes},\mathbf{M})
in
C
, it suffices to exhibit an infinite set of points thereof. For integers
q>p\geqslant 2
, set

c_{p,q}(a,b)=(e^{\frac{2\pi ip}{q}}e^{\frac{a}{2a-b}\log(3)},e^{\frac{4\pi ip}{q}}e^{\frac{b}{2a-b}\log(3)}).

These are clearly pairwise distinct points, and a direct computation shows that

c_{p,q}(a,b)\in C\cap(e^{\frac{2\pi ip}{q}},e^{\frac{4\pi ip}{q}})\cdot Y_{a,b}.

This proves the desired Zariski-density.

(ii)
We are left with proving that the full Hodge locus
\mathrm{HL}(C,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes})
is dense in
C
for the metric topology. Let
(x_{1},x_{2})=(r_{1}e^{2\pi i\theta_{1}},r_{2}e^{2\pi i\theta_{2}})\in C
with
r_{1},r_{2}>0
and
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in[0,1[
. The equation of
C
forces
r_{1}^{2}=3r_{2}
and
\theta_{2}=[2\theta_{1}]
, where for
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
we denote by
[\theta]
the unique integer translate of
\theta
lying in
[0,1[
. One can chose integers
q>p\geqslant 2
such that
\theta_{1}
is arbitrarily close of
\frac{p}{q}
which forces
e^{2\pi i\theta_{2}}
to be arbitrarily close of
e^{\frac{4\pi ip}{q}}
. Similarly, chose
(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, with
b\neq 2a
, such that
|r_{2}-3^{\frac{b}{2a-b}}|<\epsilon
for arbitrary
\epsilon>0
. Such a pair exists because
r\in\mathbb{R}\mapsto e^{r\log(3)}
is a continuous surjection onto
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and rational numbers of the form
\frac{b}{2a-b}
are dense in
\mathbb{R}
. Then

|r_{1}^{2}-3^{\frac{2a}{2a-b}}|=3|r_{2}-3^{\frac{2a}{2a-b}-1}|=3|r_{2}-3^{\frac{b}{2a-b}}|<3\epsilon

which forces
|r_{1}-3^{\frac{a}{2a-b}}|<\frac{3\epsilon}{r_{2}}
. Therefore, one can approximate
(x_{1},x_{2})
arbitrarily well using the points
c_{p,q}(a,b)
for varying parameters. Since the latter are in
\mathrm{HL}(C,\mathbb{V}^{\otimes})_{\mathrm{trans}}
, this proves the desired density for the metric topology.
∎

The likeliness condition

In this section, we work in the following setting:

Setting 7.1
.

\mathbb{V}
is an effective integral variation of mixed Hodge structures on a smooth and irreducible quasi-projective complex algebraic variety
S
. Let
\pi_{S}:\widetilde{S^{\mathrm{an}}}\rightarrow S^{\mathrm{an}}
is a universal covering map, fix a
\tilde{s}_{0}\in\widetilde{S^{\mathrm{an}}}
and
s_{0}=\pi_{S}(\tilde{s}_{0})
. We denote by

•

(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
the generic mixed Hodge class of
\mathbb{V}
and identify
\mathbf{P}
to
\mathbf{P}_{s_{0}}
and
D_{\mathbf{P}}
to
D_{\mathbf{P}_{s_{0}}}
;

•

\Gamma\subset\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{Q})^{+}
a neat arithmetic subgroup containing the image of the monodromy representation associated to
\mathbb{V}
at
s_{0}
, and
\pi_{\Gamma}:D_{\mathbf{P}}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}}),\Gamma}
the quotient map;

•

\Phi:S^{\mathrm{an}}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}:=\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}}),\Gamma}
the associated period map, with lift
\tilde{\Phi}:\widetilde{S^{\mathrm{an}}}\rightarrow D_{\mathbf{P}}
at
\tilde{s}_{0}
;

•

\mathbf{H}\triangleleft\mathbf{P}
the algebraic monodromy group of
\mathbb{V}
, and
D_{\mathbf{H}}
the weak Mumford-Tate subdomain of
D_{\mathbf{P}}
containing
\tilde{\Phi}(\widetilde{S^{\mathrm{an}}})
;

•

\mathscr{F}\subset D_{\mathbf{P}}
a semi-algebraic fundamental set for the action of
\Gamma
on
D_{\mathbf{P}}
, such that the period map
\Phi
is definable in
\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{an,exp}}
for the structure of
\mathbb{R}_{\mathrm{an,exp}}
-definable complex-analytic space on
\mathscr{D}_{(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}}),\Gamma}
induced by
\mathscr{F}
;

•

\mathscr{I}
the smooth locally closed complex-analytic (by effectivity of
\mathbb{V}
) subset
\tilde{\Phi}(\widetilde{S^{\mathrm{an}}})\cap\mathscr{F}
of
D_{\mathbf{P}}
;

•

q_{\mathbf{N}}:D_{\mathbf{P}}\rightarrow D_{\mathbf{P}}/\mathbf{N}
and by
p_{\mathbf{N}}:\mathscr{D}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}/\mathbf{N}
the quotient maps associated to a
\mathbf{N}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{P})
, and by
\Phi_{/\mathbf{N}}:S^{\mathrm{an}}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}/\mathbf{N}
the composite
p_{\mathbf{N}}\circ\Phi
.

Characterization of likeliness

In this subsection, we prove an easier to handle and equivalent formulation of the likeliness condition, in the spirit of
[
37
]
. Start with the:

Definition 7.2
.

Let
(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}})\subsetneq(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
be a strict mixed Hodge subclass and
\mathbf{N}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{P})
be a strict normal subgroup whose radical is unipotent. A pair
(g,t)\in\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{R})^{+}\mathbf{P}^{u}(\mathbb{C})\times D_{\mathbf{P}}/\mathbf{N}
is called a
(\mathscr{I},D_{\mathbf{M}},D_{\mathbf{P}}/\mathbf{N})
-intersecting pair
if

\mathscr{I}\cap(g\cdot D_{\mathbf{M}})\cap q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t)\neq\emptyset

Then we have:

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}})\subsetneq(\mathbf{P},D_{\mathbf{P}})
be a strict mixed Hodge subclass. There exists a non-empty Zariski open subset
S_{\mathrm{gen}}
of
S
such that letting
\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{gen}}=\mathscr{F}\cap\tilde{\Phi}(\pi_{S}^{-1}(S_{\mathrm{gen}}^{\mathrm{an}}))
the following holds: for every
\mathbf{N}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{P})
and any
(\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{gen}},D_{\mathbf{M}},D_{\mathbf{P}}/\mathbf{N})
-intersecting pair
(g,t)\in\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{R})^{+}\mathbf{P}^{u}(\mathbb{C})\times D_{\mathbf{P}}/\mathbf{N}
, the intersections

(a)

(g\cdot D_{\mathbf{M}})\cap q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t)

(b)

\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{gen}}\cap q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t)

are equidimensional and the dimension of each of them is independent of the choice of the
(\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{gen}},D_{\mathbf{M}},D_{\mathbf{P}}/\mathbf{N})
-intersecting pair
(g,t)
. In particular, the quantity

d_{\mathbf{N}}(\mathbf{M},D_{\mathbf{M}}):=\dim\big((g\cdot D_{\mathbf{M}})\cap q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t)\big)+\dim\big(\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{gen}}\cap q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t)\big)-\dim q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t)

is well defined and only depends on
\mathbf{N}
.

Proof.

(a)
Let
(g,t)
and
(g^{\prime},t^{\prime})
be two possibly equal
(\mathscr{I},D_{\mathbf{M}},D_{\mathbf{P}}/\mathbf{N})
-intersecting pairs. Let
x\in(g\cdot D_{\mathbf{M}})\cap q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t)
and
x^{\prime}\in(g^{\prime}\cdot D_{\mathbf{M}})\cap q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t^{\prime})
. We want to prove that

\dim_{x}(g\cdot D_{\mathbf{M}})\cap q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t)=\dim_{x^{\prime}}(g^{\prime}\cdot D_{\mathbf{M}})\cap q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t^{\prime}).

Since
g^{\prime}\mathbf{M}(\mathbb{R})^{+}\mathbf{M}^{u}(\mathbb{C})g^{\prime-1}
acts transitively on
g^{\prime}\cdot D_{\mathbf{M}}
, and since
g^{\prime}g^{-1}x\in g^{\prime}\cdot D_{\mathbf{M}}
, there exists a
m\in g^{\prime}\mathbf{M}(\mathbb{R})^{+}\mathbf{M}^{u}(\mathbb{C})g^{\prime-1}
such that
x^{\prime}=mg^{\prime}g^{-1}x
. Then,

x^{\prime}\in mg^{\prime}g^{-1}\cdot\Big((g\cdot D_{\mathbf{M}})\cap q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t)\Big)=(g^{\prime}\cdot D_{\mathbf{M}})\cap\Big(mg^{\prime}g^{-1}\cdot(q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t))\Big).

We claim that
mg^{\prime}g^{-1}\cdot(q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t))=q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t^{\prime})
. Indeed, the map
q_{\mathbf{N}}
is by construction equivariant under the quotient map
\pi_{\mathbf{N}}:\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{R})^{+}\mathbf{P}^{u}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow(\mathbf{P}/\mathbf{N})(\mathbb{R})^{+}(\mathbf{P}^{u}/\mathbf{N})(\mathbb{C})
, so that

mg^{\prime}g^{-1}\cdot(q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t))=q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(\pi_{\mathbf{N}}(mg^{\prime}g^{-1})\cdot t).

But, we saw that
x^{\prime}\in mg^{\prime}g^{-1}\cdot(q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t))
and by definition
x^{\prime}\in q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t^{\prime})
so
\pi_{\mathbf{N}}(mg^{\prime}g^{-1})\cdot t=t^{\prime}
. This proves the claim.

Conjugation by
mg^{\prime}g^{-1}
gives a biholomorphic automorphism of
D_{\mathbf{P}}
and maps biholomorphically any complex-analytic irreducible component of
(g\cdot D_{\mathbf{M}})\cap q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t)
containing
x
to some complex-analytic irreducible component of
(g^{\prime}\cdot D_{\mathbf{M}})\cap q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t^{\prime})
containing
x^{\prime}
. Choosing
(g,t)=(g^{\prime},t^{\prime})
and
x
(resp.
x^{\prime}
) such that only one complex-analytic irreducible component of
(g\cdot D_{\mathbf{M}})\cap q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t)
(resp.
(g\cdot D_{\mathbf{M}})\cap q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t)
) contains
x
(resp.
x^{\prime}
) shows the equidimensionality of
(g\cdot D_{\mathbf{M}})\cap q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t)
. Choosing
(g,t)\neq(g^{\prime},t^{\prime})
and
x,x^{\prime}
arbitrarily shows that the (well defined by equidimensionality) complex-analytic dimensions of
(g\cdot D_{\mathbf{M}})\cap q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t)
and
(g^{\prime}\cdot D_{\mathbf{M}})\cap q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t^{\prime})
are equal. This proves
(a)
, without having to replace
S
by an open subset.

(b)
Fix some
\mathbf{N}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{P})
. We start by proving that there is a non-empty Zariski open subset
S_{\mathbf{N},\mathrm{gen}}
of
S
such that
(b)
holds for
\mathbf{N}
after replacing
S
by
S_{\mathbf{N},\mathrm{gen}}
. Let
T_{\mathbf{N}}=\{t\in D_{\mathbf{P}}/\mathbf{N}\hskip 2.84544pt:\hskip 2.84544pt\mathscr{I}\cap q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t)\neq\emptyset\}
the set of second-coordinates of
(\mathscr{I},D_{\mathbf{M}},D_{\mathbf{P}}/\mathbf{N})
-intersecting pairs. Let
C_{\mathbf{N}}=\min\{\dim_{x}\mathscr{I}\cap q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t)\hskip 2.84544pt:\hskip 2.84544ptt\in T_{\mathbf{N}},x\in\mathscr{I}\cap q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t)\}
which is a well defined non-negative integer as the minimum of a set of non-negative integers. Define

\mathscr{I}_{\mathbf{N},\mathrm{ex}}=\{x\in\mathscr{I}\hskip 2.84544pt:\hskip 2.84544pt\dim_{x}\mathscr{I}\cap q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(q_{\mathbf{N}}(x))>C_{\mathbf{N}}\},

and
S_{\mathbf{N},\mathrm{ex}}=\pi_{S}(\tilde{\Phi}^{-1}(\mathscr{I}_{\mathbf{N},\mathrm{ex}}))
. We first claim that it suffices to prove that
S_{\mathbf{N},\mathrm{ex}}
is a strict Zariski-closed subset of
S
to prove
(b)
. Indeed, assume that
S_{\mathbf{N},\mathrm{ex}}
is a strict Zariski-closed subset of
S
. Replace
S
by its non-empty Zariski open subset
S_{\mathbf{N},\mathrm{gen}}:=S-S_{\mathbf{N},\mathrm{ex}}
. After this operation, the subspace
\mathscr{I}\subset D_{\mathbf{P}}
is replaced by
\mathscr{I}_{\mathbf{N},\mathrm{gen}}:=\mathscr{I}-\mathscr{I}_{\mathbf{N},\mathrm{ex}}\subset D_{\mathbf{P}}
. Let
(g,t)
and
(g^{\prime},t^{\prime})
be two possibly equal
(\mathscr{I}_{\mathbf{N},\mathrm{gen}},D_{\mathbf{M}},D_{\mathbf{P}}/\mathbf{N})
-intersecting pairs,
x\in\mathscr{I}_{\mathbf{N},\mathrm{gen}}\cap q_{\mathbf{N}}^{-1}(t)
and
